L1329: ## F. Pulmonary Safety Conclusions
L1330: DPARP's assessment of pulmonary safety focuses on a 6-month placebo-controlled trial in 258 subjects and two-active controlled trials, ~2 years in length in 1313 and 1346 subjects, respectively. The pulmonary safety analyses are further supported by uncontrolled extension periods in 2744 subjects. Pulmonary function changes were not consistent or robust in the individual trials; however, the pooled analyses did demonstrate dose-dependent changes in FEV1 and FVC as early as Month 3. The changes in FEV1 were sustained through Month 12, while the changes in FVC were not statistically significant at other timepoints. The change from baseline in absolute FEV1 and FVC at Month 3 was -60 mL (-90, -20) and -60 mL (-110, -10), respectively. The change from baseline in percent-predicted FEV1 and FVC at Month 3 was -1.63 (-2.63, -0.04) and -1.42 (-2.6, -0.21), respectively. The magnitude of change for FEV1 was sustained through Month 12. Statistically significant changes in FEV1 were not noted after Month 12, however, this is limited by the decreases in sample size after Month 12. Although FEV1 and FVC were generally stable in uncontrolled extension periods (up to 22 and 39 months), there is insufficient information to determine the reversibility of the decrease in FEV1 or FVC after drug discontinuation due to limited follow-up.
L1331: Overall, pulmonary AEs associated with ozanimod use are rare and were considered to be mostly mild or moderate. Reports of dyspnea following ozanimod use were rare and were not associated with changes in PFT parameters, including the one participant who withdrew from the OLE trial (RPC01-3001) due to dyspnea. Considering both the mild-
L1332: moderate severity and rarity of these adverse events overall, the risk of pulmonary toxicity with ozanimod use can be mitigated through labeling and patient education.
L1333: Pulmonary effects are expected based on the mechanism of action of S1P modulators and have been seen in approved S1P modulators, fingolimod and siponimod. Both fingolimod and siponimod demonstrated decreases in FEV1. In addition, fingolimod demonstrated decreases in DLCO and siponimod demonstrated decreases in FVC. The magnitude of change in pulmonary function was comparable to ozanimod. Both fingolimod and siponimod also noted several subjects discontinuing due to dyspnea compared to the ozanimod trials which also noted one subject discontinuing for dyspnea. Overall, the pulmonary safety profile for the S1P modulators are comparable.
L1334: PMRs were included in the approval of both fingolimod and siponimod. Because there are outstanding PMRs designed to monitor pulmonary toxicity with long term, chronic use of the other two other drugs in this class, fingolimod and siponimod, the utility of a third PMR is arguably limited. While it is unclear if the pulmonary effects for ozanimod are reversible, a trial designed to assess reversibility following drug discontinuation would be unethical, given the concern that stopping S1P modulators can cause worsening of MS symptoms (4-6). It is also unclear if pulmonary function will decline over time. A comparator group would be required to adequately assess pulmonary function decline over time; however, as S1P receptor modulators are considered to be part of standard of care, a comparator group may not be feasible. While a trial to assess the risk of special populations could yield additional safety information, it was shown for other S1P modulators that treatment of those with COPD and asthma are affected similarly to those without respiratory disease at doses approved for RMS treatment (5, 6). At this time, we are therefore not recommending a PMR for ozanimod, though it is possible that we would recommend the issuance of one in the setting of new clinical data from the PMR trials of fingolimod or siponimod.
L1335: ## VI. Labeling Recommendations
L1336: Based on our review of pulmonary function safety, we recommend that ozanimod, like other S1P receptor modulators, include verbiage reflecting observed changes in pulmonary function testing as well as the respiratory effects that were seen during the clinical development program, as outlined below.
L1337: In HIGHLIGHTS OF PRESCRIBING INFORMATION, under WARNINGS AND PRECAUTIONS:
L1338: - “Respiratory Effects: May cause a decline in pulmonary function. Assess pulmonary function (e.g., spirometry) if clinically indicated.”
L1339: In SECTION 5 WARNINGS AND PRECAUTIONS:
L1340: - “Dose-dependent reductions in absolute forced expiratory volume over 1 second (FEV1) were observed in patients treated with ZEPOSIA as early as 3 months after treatment initiation. In pooled analyses of [REDACTED], the decline in absolute FEV1 from baseline [REDACTED] was 60 mL (95% CI: -100, -20) at 12 months. The mean difference [REDACTED] in percent predicted FEV1 at 12 months was 1.9% (95% CI: -2.9, -0.8). Dose-dependent reductions in FVC (absolute value and %-predicted) were also seen at Month 3 in pooled analyses comparing [REDACTED] (60 mL, 95% CI (-110, -10); 1.4%, 95% CI: (-2.6, -0.2)), though significant reductions were not seen at other timepoints. There is insufficient information to determine the reversibility of the decrease in FEV1 or FVC after drug discontinuation. [REDACTED] one patient discontinued ZEPOSIA due to dyspnea. Spirometric evaluation of respiratory function should be performed during therapy with ZEPOSIA if clinically indicated.”
L1341: In SECTION 6 ADVERSE REACTIONS:
L1342: - “Dose-dependent reductions in forced expiratory volume over 1 second (FEV1) and forced vital capacity (FVC) were observed in patients treated with ZEPOSIA [see Warnings and Precautions]”
L1343: *Comment to DNP: AEs related to changes in spirometry within the Investigations SOC should be pooled together as “Pulmonary function test abnormal” and included in any AE table or description of AEs included in Section 6.*
L1344: In SECTION 12.2 PHARMACODYNAMICS:
L1345: - “Pulmonary Function: “Dose-dependent reductions in forced expiratory volume over 1 second (FEV1) and forced vital capacity (FVC) were observed in patients treated with ZEPOSIA [see Warnings and Precautions]”
L1346: In SECTION 17 PATIENT COUNSELING INFORMATION:
L1347: - “Respiratory Effects: Advise patients that they should contact their [REDACTED] if they experience new onset or worsening dyspnea [see Warnings and Precautions]”

L1357: Figure 2 displays two line graphs, Group 1 and Group 2, showing the Mean and 95% Confidence Interval (\DeltaHR \pm 95% CI) of Heart Rate (beats/min) over 24 hours relative to dose administration. The Y-axis ranges from -20 to 10 beats/min, and the X-axis ranges from 0 to 24 hours.
Group 1 (Propranolol LA):
Propranolol LA (Black line): Shows a significant decrease in HR, starting around -10 beats/min at 1 hour, reaching a minimum of approximately -15 beats/min around 4 hours, and remaining below -10 beats/min for the rest of the 24 hours.
Propranolol LA + Ozanimod 0.25 mg (Yellow line): Shows a similar decrease in HR, starting around -10 beats/min at 1 hour, reaching a minimum of approximately -15 beats/min around 4 hours, and remaining below -10 beats/min for the rest of the 24 hours.
Propranolol LA placebo + Ozanimod 0.25 mg (Blue line): Shows minimal change in HR, remaining close to 0 beats/min throughout the 24 hours.
Group 2 (Diltiazem ER):
Diltiazem ER (Black line): Shows a significant decrease in HR, starting around -5 beats/min at 1 hour, reaching a minimum of approximately -10 beats/min around 4 hours, and remaining below -5 beats/min for the rest of the 24 hours.
Diltiazem ER + Ozanimod 0.25 mg (Yellow line): Shows a similar decrease in HR, starting around -5 beats/min at 1 hour, reaching a minimum of approximately -10 beats/min around 4 hours, and remaining below -5 beats/min for the rest of the 24 hours.
Diltiazem ER placebo + Ozanimod 0.25 mg (Blue line): Shows minimal change in HR, remaining close to 0 beats/min throughout the 24 hours.
In both groups, the combination of propranolol LA or diltiazem ER with ozanimod 0.25 mg resulted in a similar magnitude of HR decrease compared to the placebo + ozanimod group, indicating no potentiation of the HR-lowering effect of the beta-blocker or calcium channel blocker by ozanimod.

RPC1063 2 mg (N=12)

The graph illustrates that heart rate generally decreases (becomes more negative) compared to placebo, with the magnitude of decrease increasing with higher doses, particularly after hour 10.
L1363: Figure 4 displays four line graphs showing the change from predose baseline in mean hourly heart rate (\DeltaHR) in beats per minute (bpm) over time (hours relative to dose) for two treatments (RPC1063 and Placebo) across four titration days (Day 1, Day 5, Day 8, Day 11).
The Y-axis represents \DeltaHR 95% CI (bpm), ranging from -25 to 15. The X-axis represents Time relative to dose (hours), ranging from 0 to 25.
Legend: RPC1063 (Black line with diamond markers), Placebo (Orange line with triangle markers).
Day 1 (0.25 mg): Both treatments show a slight decrease in HR around 5 hours, with Placebo reaching approximately -10 bpm and RPC1063 around -15 bpm. HR generally stabilizes near 0 bpm thereafter.
Day 5 (0.5 mg): Both treatments show a significant decrease in HR around 5 hours, with Placebo reaching approximately -15 bpm and RPC1063 around -20 bpm. HR generally stabilizes near 0 bpm thereafter.
Day 8 (1 mg): Both treatments show a significant decrease in HR around 5 hours, with Placebo reaching approximately -15 bpm and RPC1063 around -20 bpm. HR generally stabilizes near 0 bpm thereafter.
Day 11 (2 mg): Both treatments show a significant decrease in HR around 5 hours, with Placebo reaching approximately -15 bpm and RPC1063 around -20 bpm. HR generally stabilizes near 0 bpm thereafter.
In all four panels, the RPC1063 treatment consistently shows a greater decrease in mean hourly heart rate compared to the Placebo treatment, particularly around 5 hours post-dose.
L1364: Figure 5 displays a line graph showing the difference of the mean change from baseline in heart rate (beats/minute) compared to Placebo over time (hours relative to dose) for four titration days (Day 1, Day 5, Day 8, Day 11).
The Y-axis represents \DeltaHR 95% CI (bpm), ranging from -25 to 15. The X-axis represents Time relative to dose (hours), ranging from 0 to 25.
Legend: Day 1 (0.25 mg) (Black line with diamond markers), Day 5 (0.5 mg) (Orange line with triangle markers), Day 8 (1 mg) (Blue line with square markers), Day 11 (2 mg) (Green line with circle markers).
The graph illustrates the difference in mean change from baseline HR compared to Placebo. The difference is calculated as \DeltaHR (Ozanimod) - \DeltaHR (Placebo).
At Time 0 (predose), the difference is 0 bpm for all doses.
At approximately 5 hours post-dose, the difference is most pronounced:
Day 1 (0.25 mg): Difference is approximately -5 bpm.
Day 5 (0.5 mg): Difference is approximately -10 bpm.
Day 8 (1 mg): Difference is approximately -15 bpm.
Day 11 (2 mg): Difference is approximately -10 bpm.
The magnitude of the difference generally decreases as the dose increases from Day 1 to Day 11, although Day 8 (1 mg) shows the largest difference at 5 hours. The differences generally stabilize near 0 bpm after 10 hours.
L1365: Line graph showing the Difference of the Mean Change from Baseline in Hourly Heart Rate (ΔHR, 95% CI in bpm) versus Time relative to dose (hours) for four different days (doses) of ozanimod administration. The Y-axis ranges from -25 to 15 bpm. The X-axis ranges from 0 to 25 hours. L1366: Figure 7 is a line graph showing Mean Hourly Heart Rate (\pm SE) on Day 8 in Study RPC01-201A. The Y-axis represents Mean (SE) HR (bpm) from 50 to 90. The X-axis represents Hour from 0 to 24. Three groups are plotted: Placebo (N=25, blue line), RPC1063 0.5 mg (N=25, red line), and RPC1063 1 mg (N=25, green line). All groups show a similar pattern of heart rate, starting around 75-80 bpm, peaking around 2-4 hours, dropping significantly around 14-16 hours (Placebo around 58 bpm, RPC1063 0.5 mg around 55 bpm, RPC1063 1 mg around 60 bpm), and recovering by 24 hours (Placebo around 78 bpm, RPC1063 0.5 mg around 75 bpm, RPC1063 1 mg around 75 bpm). Error bars represent \pm SE.

L1368: Figure 1 illustrates the study design of RPC01-201A, divided into a Placebo-Controlled portion and a Blinded Extension portion, plotted against the Week of Total Trial (from -4 to 24, and extending into the Blinded Extension phase).
The study begins with a 30-Day Screening Period, followed by Randomization. Randomization leads to three groups during the 24-Week Placebo-Controlled Treatment Period:
Ozanimod 1 mg
Ozanimod 0.5 mg
Placebo
Each group is preceded by a 7-Day Dose-Escalation Period. After the 24-Week Placebo-Controlled Treatment Period, the study enters the Blinded Extension phase. During the Blinded Extension, participants continue treatment:
Ozanimod 1 mg (Continuation from Ozanimod 1 mg group)
Ozanimod 0.5 mg (Continuation from Ozanimod 0.5 mg group)
Ozanimod 1 mg (Continuation from Placebo group, indicated by hatched pattern)
Ozanimod 0.5 mg (Continuation from Placebo group, indicated by hatched pattern)
L1369: Figure 2 is a timeline illustrating the Study Design of RPC01-201B. The study is divided into Part A (implied, preceding Part B) and Part B. Part B begins with a 30-day Screening period, starting on Day 1 (Randomization). During Screening, subjects undergo enrollment initiation after completion of Part A, and DMC reviews interim data. Following Screening, subjects enter the Active-controlled Period, which lasts until Month 24 (Part B: Month 24). During the Active-controlled Period, subjects are randomized to receive either 1 mg ozanimod, 0.5 mg ozanimod, or IFN\beta-1a. The Part B Primary Endpoint is ARR (Annualized Relapse Rate).
